Frederick A. Smith, son of Elder Alexander H. Smith, and Elizabeth Agnes (Kendall) Smith was born in Sonora 

Township, Hancock County, Illinois, January 19, 1862, and baptized June 24, 1877. He served successively in the following offices: deacon (July 9, 1882), priest (June 3, 1890), elder (April 14, 1891), seventy (April 15, 1894), high priest (April 19, 1900), apostle (April 19, 1902), presiding patriarch of the church (April 20, 1913). Brother

Smith became Presiding Patriarch Emeritus at the conference of 1938. 

Francis M. Sheehy was born June 1, 1851, and baptized 

January 29, 1871. He was ordained a deacon September, [ 429]

1872; a teacher April, 1873; a priest May, 1874; an elder 

April, 1876; a seventy April, 1886; president of the seventy 

April, 1897; a high priest April, 1900 and an apostle April, 1902. Honorably released from active service in the apostolic quorum in April, 1920, he died October 17, 1923.

Most of Brother Sheehy's missionary work was in the eastern states, although he also saw service in the western and central territories. Elder Sheehy was one of the first members of the church to take a deep and intelligent interest in American archaeology. 

U. W. Greene was born June 16, 1865, in Massachusetts and baptized August 10, 1882, entering into the ministry soon after his baptism. Brother Greene has served in the following offices in the priesthood: deacon (September 15, 1882), priest (December 14, 1884), elder (January 15, 1888), seventy (April 8, 1890), apostle (April 20, 1902), evangelist (March 26, 1922). He died in Independence, Missouri, in 1938. 

C. A. Butterworth was born in Iowa in 1864. He was ordained an elder in 1888 and appointed to Australia at the April conference of 1889, landed in Sidney, Australia,

June 28. Ordained a seventy in 1890, he did outstanding work in that office and was called to the Twelve by the revelation of 1902, and ordained to that office by Alexander H. Smith who was in Australia at that time. He made his home in Australia, and devoted himself to the interests of the work in that far-off land. Released by the revelation of 1922 on account of ill health, he continued to serve as his strength permitted until his death September 1, 1928. 

John W. Rushton was born at Leeds, England, February 

12, 1874, and joined the church there September 15, 1888.

He served as priest, elder, and seventy in England, and after his ordination to the Apostolic Quorum was in charge or joint charge of the mission until 1911, when he located in the United States. Elder Rushton is now the senior member of the 

Quorum of Twelve. [430]

Frederick Madison Smith was a son of Joseph and 

Bertha (Madison) Smith, and was born January 21, 1874, at Plano, Illinois. He married Ruth Lyman Cobb, August 

3, 1897. Ordained to the eldership 1897, he served for a time as church librarian, assistant church historian, and as counselor to William Anderson, bishop of Lamoni Stake. 

He was ordained counselor to his father, the president of the church, in April, 1902, and became president of the church May 5, 1915. President Smith made many major and permanent contributions to the growth of the church, notably in the field of education and in the refinement of worship. He died at the Independence Sanitarium, March 20, 1946. 

R. C. Evans (see notes on section 124).

AT 

SECTION 127 T THE PRE-CONFERENCE sessions held in April, 1906, the 

Presidency presented a recommendation to the Quorum of Twelve concerning the establishment of a sanitarium and the appointment of Dr. Joseph Luff of the Quorum of

Twelve as its presiding officer. On this recommendation the Quorum of Twelve took the following action:

Resolved, that while we favor the general sentiment of the document presented by the First Presidency relating to the medical work of Brother Joseph Luff and the establishment of a hospital, we are, nevertheless, uncertain as to whether such work is consistent with said brother's calling as a member of this quorum. 

Believing, as we do, that Brother Luff was called of God to be an apostle, we would much prefer to know what is the will of God in this matter before we express ourselves upon it, lest we move unwisely. We would therefore, gladly engage in an effort to learn the Master's will concerning it and other matters should you approve of it. 

The inquiries resulting from this action led to the revelation addressed to the church presented to the conference 

April 14, 1906, at Independence, Missouri. It may be [431]

noted that President Joseph Smith was in his seventy-fourth year at this time, and that there was widespread concern regarding the manner of choosing his successor. Undoubtedly, therefore, this matter also had been frequently mentioned in the prayers of the Saints. "It is the will of the Lord that a sanitarium, a place of refuge and health for the sick and afflicted be established by the church at Independence, Missouri" (1). In accord with this instruction, Bishop E. L. Kelley purchased a tract of land in Independence which came to be known as the "Kensington Addition," and on a part of this the Sanitarium was located. Excavation for the building began in August, 1907, and it was formally opened December 15, 1909, entirely free from debt. An additional modern building is now almost completed, six of the seven floors being in use. 

The sanitarium is both a medical center and a training school for nurses. "The presiding bishop and his counselors and the bishopric of the Independence Stake should take counsel together in locating and establishing the sanitarium" (1). Note the principle involved here. The matter of locating and establishing the sanitarium had to do with the physical properties of the church. It was therefore properly referred to the 

Bishopric. However, the matter was both a general and a local concern, and so the general and local bishopric were associated in the enterprise. Elder Roderick May was at this time Bishop of the Independence Stake. "It is also expedient that these should be assisted by the advice and counsel of one of my servants who is acquainted with the laws of health and the practice of medicine" (2). This reasonable provision for the advice of an expert was fulfilled by the appointment of Joseph Luff, M. D., of the 

Quorum of Twelve. "That he may be an assistant to those who seek the aid of this institution of the church, in his spiritual office and his calling as a physician" (2). This intelligent association of faith [432]

and specialized training in ministering to the sick is in entire harmony with the early practice of the church.¹ In this connection we call attention to the following quotation from the writings of John Wesley:

I earnestly advise every one, together with all other medicines, to use that medicine of medicines—prayer. Where is the cure for either lingering or impetuous passions that either furiously overturn this house of earth, or sap the fundations of health and life by sure approaches? 

The whole materia Medica is of no avail in this case. What can cure it but the peace of God? No other medicine under heaven. What but the love of God, that sovereign balm for the body as well as the mind. 

The passions have a greater influence on health than most people are aware of. All violent and sudden passions dispose to, or actually throw people into acute diseases. The slow and lasting passions, such as grief or hopeless love, bring on chronic diseases. Till the passion which caused the disease is calmed, medicine is applied in vain. The love of God, as it is the sovereign remedy of all miseries, so in particular it effectually prevents all the bodily disorders the passions introduce, by keeping the passions themselves within due bounds. And by the unspeakable joy, and perfect calm, serenity and tranquillity it gives the mind, it becomes the most powerful of all the means of health and long life. "There shall also be a home for children established" (3).

The Daughters of Zion, an organization among the women of the church, raised a fund for the erection of this home and requested the conference of 1907 to authorize them to appoint a committee to act with the Bishopric in completing this work when such completion should be deemed advisable. 

The conference concurred, and the sisters appointed Sisters 

B. C. Smith, Callie B. Stebbins, Ruth L. Smith, Evaline Burgess, and Emma Hougas. The home was located at Lamoni, Iowa, and was formally opened August 15, 1907. "Debt should not be contracted nor too large nor expensive buildings be built at the outset" (4). In this connection Bishop 

Kelley reported to the conference of 1909 as follows:

The Sanitarium was built with funds raised especially for that purpose, and from church tithes and offerings also. We need not have sent out and asked a single special contribution for the Sanitarium, if we [433]

had not wished to do so. The authority is with the Bishopric to have built it from the tithe and offering fund. But since we did not have sufficient of the tithe and offering fund, we made special collections for that, and it has been builded, and not one cent against it, nor has the church been involved in debt by reason of building it.' "The mission to Utah and the west should be continued" (6). . One of the difficulties of missionary work in Utah was that conditions in Utah were made so unfavorable for persons joining the Reorganization that the majority of those converted moved out within a short time and located on the coast or nearer the center place. It has always been difficult to maintain a working nucleus in this area, although many have joined the church in Utah.

The Gathering (7). During the early years of his administration considerable pressure was brought to bear on President 

Joseph Smith, urging him to sound the call to gather. With a wisdom which was in advance of his experience, he resisted this pressure until the years solved problems which would have brought division if the Saints had gathered earlier. In the late eighties and nineties, however, many of these difficulties had been overcome, and at the turn of the century, the ordination of local bishops marked a definite stage in emphasis upon the gathering. When the enthusiasm and energetic leadership of President Frederick M. Smith was added to the statesmanship of his venerable father, interest in the necessity and value of the gathering grew apace. It was necessary, however, that the errors of an earlier generation should not be repeated, and the revelation of 1906 therefore gave some fundamental and timely instruction regarding the gathering. 

Note the following principles: 

The gathering must not be in haste.

It must be in harmony with the revelation on Fishing River.³ 

The Saints must learn to be obedient to the things which the Lord requires at their hands.

Zion cannot be built up unless it is by the principles of the law of the celestial kingdom. [434]

The full preparation of the priesthood must await their more complete endowment.*

The Saints should gather only after consultation with the Presidency and Bishopric, as provided in the law. 

The spirit of speculation and of greed for gain should be avoided.

Those preaching and writing upon the gathering should be careful to remove the principle of selfishness from their appeal. "My servant Frederick M. Smith, if he remain faithful and steadfast, should be chosen" (8). Frederick Madison Smith was more fully designated in the "Letter of Instruction" issued by President Joseph Smith, March 4, 1912. He was ordained president of the high priesthood, and prophet, seer, and revelator of the church May 5, 1915, by Gomer T. Griffiths, president of the Quorum of Twelve, assisted by Apostle Peter Anderson Bishop E. L. Kelley, president of the Aaronic priesthood, and 

Elder Joseph A. Tanner, president of the Quorum of High Priests.

SECTION 128 

IN 

N HIS REPORT to the General Conference of 1909, Presiding 

Bishop E. L. Kelley included the following paragraph: 

The church in its work of preparation and arrangement has arrived at the place where in the opinion of the Bishopric we must consider the question of carrying into effect the law relating to the purchase of land for the good of the work and the settlement properly, of the Saints. 

There is another matter, however, that should in the opinion of the Bishop, be taken up and passed upon before largely entering upon this work, that it presents the conditions so special to the business interests of the church, that it would be preferable to first present the same before a special meeting of the elders, under the supervision of [ 435]

the Presidency, or present to the Joint Council; but since the action of the body will be required subsequently, it would save time by going before the eldership, where it could be heard and first passed on. 

On Tuesday, April 20, the Joint Council of the Melchisedec priesthood presented the following report: 

To the General Conference: At a meeting of the eldership of the church called by the Bishop April 9, he requested that organizations be formed to hold properties for the benefit of the poor of the church. A motion prevailed to refer this request to the Presidency of the church for instruction. 

April 17, the Presidency called another meeting of the eldership, at which time the President requested the support and prayers of the ministry present. In accord with this request a day of fasting and prayer was held by them on the 18th instant. 

On April 19, another meeting was called by the Presidency, and there was presented an answer to the request for instruction upon the subject under consideration purporting to be from the Lord. 

The document was presented to the several quorums of the eldership for consideration and action, and favorable reports were received by all, when a motion prevailed, "That this council report its action to the General Conference;" which action was, "That the document be adopted as a whole."

The revelation referred to in the report is addressed to the eldership and dated Lamoni, Iowa, April 18, 1909. "The conditions surrounding the work" (1). This seems to be a prophetic reference to the coming of the recent years of economic unrest. A major tragedy 

A major tragedy of the war years and the intervening period has been the absence of any Zionic area in which the Saints could work out their spiritual purposes for their own salvation and the enlightenment of humanity. "The increase of the membership of the church" (1). At this time the church numbered almost sixty thousand members. "Under the existing laws" (1). The laws of the state and nation are properly designed to protect the interests of the individual against possible predatory purposes on the part [436]

of other persons or organizations. This is as it should be; and the gathering of the Saints must be in harmony with these laws, or other laws must be enacted which recognize the spiritual purposes of our association. "The custodians of the properties of the church" (2). This phrase is explained more fully in the succeeding paragraphs and refers to "the presiding bishop and his counselors together with the other bishops of the church." In "the work of caring for and using" properties in any particular locality, the Presiding Bishopric and the local bishopric will be directly associated. This is in harmony with the principles followed in the location and establishment of the sanitarium. ¹ 1、 "Consult with the elders and the bishops before removing" (4). This frequently repeated counsel must be observed if the gathering is to proceed in an orderly way and without disrupting the work of the church in more distant fields. 

It is not enough that a family wishing to move to Independence or the regions round about shall be spiritually and financially able. It is necessary that their removal shall not create too great a hardship in the branch or district from which they are transferred. "Regions round about" (5). This area would certainly include the territory covered by the present stakes plus land as near to Independence but lying to the south and west. "They cannot withdraw themselves" (8). The associations of the Saints are designed for purposes of mutual helpfulness. 

These associations cannot be independent but must have some contacts with the surrounding world. Yet these contacts should be of a high ethical and spiritual order and so should quicken the admiration of our neighbors and bear testimony to them of the high quality of our endeavors. The Saints cannot descend to some of the business practices current in the world. The revelation of 19062 is particularly significant in this connection. "The general authorities of the church who are made the proper counselors in spiritual and temporal things" (9). It [ 437 ]

will be noted that this entire revelation carefully safeguards the prerogatives of the Bishopric as those "made the custodians of the properties of the church." Before its conclusion, however, it requires these financial leaders to consult with the general authorities of the church in matters of major importance. This is in harmony with the general principle of the subordination of temporalities to the achievement of spiritual purposes and 3 with earlier revelation. ³ 

¹Section 127: 1. Section 127: 7. Section 122: 6. 

SECTION 129 EDNESDAY, April 14, 1909 while the problems introduced by the Presiding Bishop were still under consideration by the Joint Council of the Melchisedec priesthood, the following resolution was adopted by the General Conference: 

Desiring to place ourselves in such attitude as an assembly before our Heavenly Father, such as to especially commend ourselves to him, and thereby invite his special blessing and immediate divine direction to be given to us in his wisdom and pleasure. 

Resolved, that tomorrow be set apart as a day of fasting and prayer, to be so observed as far as practicable by the assembly and the Saints.

Five days later, at the business session of April 19, 1909, President F. M. Smith stated that by instruction of President

Joseph Smith, he presented to the body a document containing a communication to the church, with the recommendation that it be referred to the various quorums for consideration and action. At the business session of April 20, after reports had been received indicating that the various quorums accepted the revelation as of divine origin, it was moved "that we accept the document as a revelation from God and pledge ourselves to be goverened by it." The motion was adopted by unanimous vote. [438]

This revelation is dated April 18, 1909, at Lamoni, Iowa, and is addressed to the church. "It is no longer wise that my servant R. C. Evans be continued as counselor in the presidency" (1). By reason of certain administrative difficulties, the members of the Quorum of Twelve already had notified the First Presidency of their inability to sustain Elder Evans as a member of the First 

Presidency. Brother Evans was released from the Presidency in harmony with the instruction of this revelation and later in the conference was ordained a bishop. "Release him [Joseph Luff] from the responsibilities of the active apostleship as a member of the quorum, that he may act unreservedly in his calling" (2). At the time of Doctor 

Luff's selection as church physician, the members of the Quorum of Twelve had been doubtful whether he could carry that responsibility in addition to his apostolic duties. 

In reply the Lord had stated that Brother Luff could function in both capacities. ¹ The growth of his responsibilities at the 

Sanitarium, however, now made necessary the change recommended in this paragraph. Doctor Luff continued as church physician until 1918, when he was succeeded by Doctor G. 

Leonard Harrington and Doctor A. W. Teel. The present church physician is Doctor Charles F. Grabske. 1 "Holding the high priesthood" (4). Members of the Presidency, Twelve, Order of Bishops, and Order of Evangelists are all high priests set apart to a special work. When they are relieved from responsibility connected with these special callings, the members of these groups revert to their former standing as high priests. "The attention of the church is called to the consideration of the revelation given in answer to earnest supplication, with regard to temporal things" (8). This revelation was given at the same conference but addressed to the eldership. 2 "The bishopric, men holding office of bishop under a presiding head" (8). In stakes, districts, and large branches, where bishops are appointed and ordained, they rightfully [ 439]

take charge of the temporal concerns of these groups. The 

Presiding Bishopric preside over the bishops of the church in temporal things and in addition have direct charge of the temporal concerns of the general church. They also appoint agents in those branches and districts where no bishops are ordained and set in their places. Thus the bishopric-men holding the office of bishop under a presiding head—gather, care for and disperse the monies and temporal properties of the entire church. "The church has been directed to accede to the rendition of the Bishopric with respect to the temporal law" (8). Read section 126: 10 in this connection. "Until such heed is paid to the word which has been given the church cannot receive and enjoy the blessings which have been looked for when Zion shall be fully redeemed" (8). The basic principle underlying this instruction is found in section 125:14.

If my people will respect the officers whom I have called and set in the church, I will respect these officers, and if they do not, they cannot expect the riches of gifts and the blessings of direction. 

This applies to the Presidency, whose duty it is to teach the revelations and to co-ordinate the activities of the various departments of church work. It applies to the Twelve, who are "the chief missionaries of the church." It applies to the 

Bishopric who are in charge of "the temporalities of the church." It applies also to the other officers and ministers of the church in their several callings. Due care should, of course, be taken to see that the rights of the church are properly safeguarded, and there is ample provision in the law for this safeguarding. If the priesthood is to be effective in the life of the church, then the ministry must be supported in discharging their duty. "The Lord is well pleased" (9). It will be noted that this commendation is received despite the differences of opinion which had been held during the preceding confer- [ 440 ]

ence year, because these differences had been "held in unity of purpose and desire for the good of my people" and would therefore "result in bringing to pass a unity of understanding." It was on this background that the elders and the 

Saints generally were told "so be ye encouraged and press on to the consummation designed of God for his people-unity, honor, sanctification, and glory."

BIOGRAPHICAL NOTES 

Elbert A. Smith, son of David Hyrum and Clara Charlotte 

Hartshorn Smith, was born March 8, 1871, at Nauvoo, Illinois. In 1876 he moved with his mother to a farm in western Iowa, and in 1892 to Lamoni, where he was a printer and bookbinder in the Herald Publishing House. He married 

Clara, daughter of Asa Cochran, in Lamoni, September 4, 1895. Entering the ministry in early manhood, Brother 

Smith has served as pastor of the congregation at Burlington, Iowa, assistant and associate editor of the Saints' Herald for over twenty years, and editor of Autumn Leaves for a similarly long period. Ordained second counselor to President Joseph 

Smith, his uncle, in 1909, he was ordained first counselor to 

President Frederick Madison Smith, May 5, 1915, and Presiding Patriarch of the church, April 10, 1938. "Father" Smith is widely and affectionately known throughout the church. He and Sister Smith have two living sons, Ronald Gibson and

Lynn Elbert, both of whom are members of the Melchisedec priesthood. 

James F. Curtis was born January 26, 1875, in Livingston

County, Missouri. He was baptized June 10, 1883. In July, 1899, Brother Curtis was ordained an elder and in April, 1903, a seventy. Six years later he was called to the apostolic office and served as a member of the Quorum of Twelve until his ordination to the evangelical order, April 10, 1938. 

Robert Clark Russell was born August 23, 1867, in Dundalk, Ontario, Canada. On September 16, 1894, he became [441]

a member of the church and on December 3 of the same year was ordained a teacher. June 21, 1897, he was ordained a priest and in June, 1901, an elder. Four years afterwards in 

June he was set apart as a seventy, and in April, 1909, as an apostle. In October, 1922, he became an evangelist. He died 

February 5, 1929.

'HE REVELATION of April 14, 1913, was received at Lamoni, Iowa, and is addressed to the elders and the members of the conference assembled there. "Stand with their associates among the high priests and patriarchs of the church" (2). Elder W. H. Kelley continued to serve as a high priest until his death. Elders I. N. White and J. W. Elder Joseph R. Lambert acted as presiding patriarch between the death of Alexander H. Smith, August 12, 1909, and the ordination of Frederick A. Smith. 1 "The twelve in its reorganization for its work may choose its own officers" (4). The presidents of the Quorum of 

Twelve have been designated on several occasions. ¹ At this time Elder Gomer T. Griffiths was chosen to succeed Elder

A. Blakeslee was presiding bishop from 1882 to 1890. [ 442 ]

Note the instruction regarding temporalities in paragraph 7. It applies both to individuals and to the body at large: 

Avoid the unnecessary building of houses of worship or places of entertainment.

Avoid expending tithes and offering in that which may not be essential to the continued onward progress of the general work.

Carry into active exercise the principle of sacrifice and the repression of unnecessary wants.

Permit the accumulation of tithes and offerings to discharge the indebtedness of the body. 

In view of subsequent developments no comment is needed on the prophetic nature of this instruction. "The sons of the leading officers of the church are called and may be chosen to the respective offices to which the 

Spirit may direct" (9). This revelation contains four illustrations of the application of this law of lineage. Frederick A. Smith succeeded his father, Alexander H. Smith, as presiding patriarch; James E. Kelley succeeded his father, Elder William H. Kelley, in the Quorum of Twelve; James A.

Gillen succeeded his father, Elder James W. Gillen, in the 

Quorum of Twelve; and Elder E. A. Blakeslee succeeded his father, Elder George A. Blakeslee, in the Presiding Bishopric.

BIOGRAPHICAL NOTES 

James E. Kelley was born June 11, 1879, at Madison,

Indiana, and baptized April 13, 1896, at Kirtland. He was ordained an elder, July 7, 1901, a seventy April 16, 1908, and an apostle April 19, 1913. He occupied here until his death, June 4, 1917. Elder Kelley's health was poor for several years, but he served liberally according to his strength. 

William M. Aylor, who was born January 9, 1864, and baptized December 1, 1891, was ordained an elder April 13, 1898, a seventy three years later, and an apostle at the conference of 1913. He served until his resignation from the [443]

Quorum of Twelve in April, 1920, and died March 28, 1928. 

Paul M. Hanson, born January 8, 1878, at Crescent, Iowa, became a member of the church, October 15, 1893. Three years later he was ordained a priest. In 1902 he was called to the eldership and in less than a month to the seventies. 

On April 19, 1913, Brother Hanson was ordained an apostle and is still active in that office. He was ordained President of the Quorum of Twelve at the General Conference of 1934. 

James Arthur Gillen was born May 24, 1868, at Malad,

Idaho. On May 21, 1876, he became a member of the church. In September, 1900, he was ordained an elder, in 

April, 1909, a high priest, and in April, 1913, an apostle. He was selected as President of the Quorum of Twelve, October 19, 1922, and served in that capacity until his resignation from the Twelve in April, 1934. Brother Gillen died February 11, 1946, at Independence, Missouri, after a long illness. 'Sections 117: 7; 120: 8; 124: 3; 134.

SECTION 131 THE "HE REVELATION of April 5, 1914, the last received by the church through President Joseph Smith III, was addressed to the officers, delegates, and members of the Reorganized 

Church of Jesus Christ of Latter Day Saints in conference assembled. The conference for this year was held at Independence, Missouri. 

The revelation was presented to the conference Wednesday,

April 15, with the endorsement of the various quorums of the church. After it had been approved by them and the conference, President Joseph Smith made a statement, from which the following is cited:

Brother President: It would have been manifestly improper for me to have spoken to the adoption or rejection of this document, for no [444]

one knows better than I, who stand in the presence of God today, I shall have to answer for the genuine character of that which was presented, and shall be judged as one who stands upon the honesty of purpose and the integrity of duty to be performed. Called, as has been acknowledged by the church, by divine authority, under which the church originated, and under which it has always existed, and that call and the investiture of the right to act having been received from the people of the church in solemn assembly and acquiesced in for fifty-three years, I have a right to believe that as I stand to answer before God for my honesty and uprightness of intent and purpose and for the integrity of duty which I may have performed under this calling, knowing full well that I stand nearer to the answering of my duty before God than perhaps any other man in this assembly (with perhaps two or three exceptions); hence I may say with confidence that I feel very grateful indeed to God and very grateful to the people composing the church and its representatives here for the kindness of their treatment to me in the discharge of this duty of answering to the call of the people in imploring divine direction and in transmitting what I received through the Spirit which directs the work unto those who represent the body at large.

The difficulties rise up before me, and it is necessary, and it always has been for me to take a careful survey of the whole field, so far as my ability enables me to do, and when that is done, to transmit faithfully, the intent and purpose and the will of the Master, who has so far carried the work, in his wisdom, and has not as yet permitted it to meet with defeat or disaster. Hence, I would, if I could, put within the mind, -and I may add, the soul-of everyone present today, what I have felt of the difficulty of the situation under which I was called to act, and of that which I have felt in discharging that duty. For the wisdom of it, I do not answer; for the reason of it, any portion of it, I do not answer; I know not, beyond what is given, what may have been in the divine mind, and should any one of you ask me about this or that in any of the revelations which I have been permitted to give to the church, I must answer, inevitably, unless the revelation itself contains a reason, or reasons for them, I am not able to answer. I am but an humble instrument in the hands of God, as are all of you; I claim no higher tribute, no higher characteristic or qualifications of honesty, of intent or purpose, than each one of you may claim. I am not to be considered as within the counsel of the Almighty, only so far as that counsel is transmitted by the voice of inspiration to me, when action on my part is required. I dare not listen to the cries of disaster, of loss or damage.

I dare not life up my heart in triumph at apparent conquest over enemies that oppose the church. I can only, as the rest of you, do that duty that [ 445 ]

lies nearest to me, and which I am expected to perform, and leave the result in the hands of God; and brethren, before him, if he does not know his duty, and has not had the ability to perform it, I am sure, so far as I am concerned, he has chosen weak instrumentality. Whether or not, individuals called thus, choose to serve the church or refuse, it lies with them; if they refuse to receive the word of God, that which must ensue, lies with us; if we do, and we are blessed as we have been hitherto in many instances by following the divine mind, we surely are justified in a large degree for still making appeal to him, and trusting to that which he may give us. "Bishops Edwin A. Blakeslee and Edmund L. Kelley should be more closely associated in the carrying on of the financial affairs of the church" (2). At this time Bishop Kelley was in his seventieth year. Bishop Blakeslee was the first counselor in the Presiding Bishopric of the Reorganization to hold the office of bishop, having been ordained in harmony with section 130: 5. The evident intention was that Bishop 

Blakeslee should succeed Bishop Kelley as Presiding Bishop of the church. After the release of Bishop Kelley in 1916, however, Bishop Blakeslee preferred not to accept this responsibility, and Elder Benjamin R. McGuire therefore was named in his stead. The closing paragraph of the statement just quoted is significant in this connection. 1 The intention of paragraph 3 of this revelation seems to have been that Richard C. Kelley should be associated with his father and Bishop Blakeslee as the remaining member of the Presiding Bishopric. After the revelation had been accepted, Brother Richard Kelley asked for further time for consideration of his proposed ordination. is "The spirit of distrust and want of confidence unbecoming" (4). In this connection please note the following from a communication given through President Elbert A. 

Smith at the Stone Church, Independence, Missouri, April 4, 1920: 

You have been told in the past that in your public and private ministrations you should avoid sowing the seeds of distrust and division. [446]

That which a man sows, that shall he also reap. If any man shall sow discontent he shall reap discontent and be unhappy. If any man shall sow doubt he shall reap doubt in his own heart, and shall be fortunate if he shall not actually deny the faith. But he who sows the seeds of faith shall reap faith, and it shall cause his soul to enlarge and expand. 

He who gives forth love-love shall come back to him. He who is very tender and merciful, to him will I be tender and merciful, but if any man will betray my cause, him will I judge. He who puts the trust of my people in jeopardy for a little thing he himself will be in jeopardy. "The church has been admonished heretofore in this respect" (4). The last word of revelation coming to the church through Joseph Smith, its president and prophet for more than fifty-four years, pleads with the church to exorcise the spirit of distrust and want of confidence, and to support those who have been chosen as leaders. 2 [ 447 ]

COMM.

A Students' Guide to the COMMENTARY 

on the

Doctrine and Covenants By F. Henry Edwards 

This Guide consists of fifty-two lesson outlines and helps for a year of lesson studies in the revelations contained in sections 1 to 131, inclusive, of the Doctrine and Covenants. 

The lessons are planned as a guide to students and teachers to accompany the use of the text, A Commentary on the Doctrine and Covenants by the same author. 

Published for

Department of Religious Education 

Reorganized Church of Jesus Christ of Latter Day Saints 

HERALD PUBLISHING HOUSE 

103 South Osage Street 

Independence, Missouri 

Printed in the United States of America

The references to all footnotes will be found on page 59.

Introduction: 

LESSON ONE 

Divine Revelation (Introduction)

The purpose of this lesson is to show that divine revelation is a progressive movement which is still going forward, and that the record of revelation in the Bible points towards such a further record as the book of Doctrine and Covenants. 

Major Emphases:

All creation reveals its Creator. . . . True understanding of divinity awaits our full growth. . . . Meanwhile God communicates Himself to us as fully

The record of this revelation is contained in the Scriptures. 

The Scriptures are still growing. as He can.

1. Do you agree that "God is revealed everywhere and in all things"? 

Discuss this briefly. 

2. In what sense has the process of revelation come to its culmination in the life of Jesus? In what sense is it still going forward? Explain your answers. 

3. What part does God play in divine revelation? What part does man play? What is the relation between revelation and discovery? 

4. Can a man who is at enmity against God fully understand the divine. purpose? Give reasons for your answer. What is the relation between character and insight? 

5. In what sense is religion a solitary adventure? In what sense is it a social experience? In what sense is it a historical experience? 

6. Why is revelation adapted to our growth? Why is there no static finality in religion? 

7. What is the distinction between revelation and the record of revelation? What are the limitations of the record of revelation? What is the influence of sin on our capacity to understand? 

8. Give your reasons for belief in continuous revelation. Why is it advisable that the record of this revelation shall be written and become part of the Scriptures?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

The progressive revelation of God and of His will for the community should be made available as part of the inspired record. There should be a parallel growth of understanding in our individual lives, made possible by our growth in the likeness of God. Such a growing revelation awaits our growing response to the beckonings of divinity. 3

Introduction: 

LESSON TWO Publishing the Revelations (Introduction) 

This lesson seeks to present the Doctrine and Covenants as a book, as a means toward the better understanding of the individual sections. 

Major Emphases:

The scope and limitations of the Book of Commandments . . . the background of the Doctrine and Covenants analysis of the revelations. 

1. How widely were the first copies of the revelations circulated? Why is it not likely that they were more widely circulated? 

2. Who was W. W. Phelps? What important work did he do in the church? Where did he first publish the revelations? How long did this continue? 

3. Who were appointed "stewards over the revelations"? Why? What was their first attempt to discharge their responsibility? 

4. What was the purpose of the Book of Commandments? What was its extent? Why was it not completed? 

5. What were the most significant steps leading to the publication of the 

Doctrine and Covenants in 1835? What were the contents of this book? 

6. When was the second edition published, and by whom? What additions were made? Name some important subsequent editions? 

7. What editions have been issued by the Reorganized church? How many revelations are contained in the present edition of the Doctrine and 

Covenants? What other material is contained in this edition? 

8. Why does the Doctrine and Covenants contain so many early revelations addressed to individuals? Why are no recent revelations of this type included? Why are so many of the revelations dated prior to 1834?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

Both the number and the quality of the early revelations were directly influenced by the eager expectancy of the Saints and by the importance of their pioneering in kingdom building. Recovery of this expectancy and renewed activity in the specific work of the kingdom will create both the need and the atmosphere for further divine guidance.

Introduction: 

LESSON THREE The Preface 

(Section 1) The preface announces the authority, need and purpose of the revelations and indicates something of the principles which they emphasize. It should be clearly understood, however, that more than sixty of the revelations which come after this one in the book come before it in time. 

Major Emphases:

The Note of Authority. . . The Sequence of Apostasy.. 

for the Restoration . . . The Purpose of the Revelations 

Warning.

1. When was "The Preface" given? mediate circumstances caused it to be given? of the Doctrine and Covenants? . 

The Reason 

The Voice of 

Where was it given? What im 

Why is it printed at the beginning 

2. In what different senses are these revelations authoritative? What additional authority do they now have above what they had when they were first published? 

3. Discuss, briefly, the statement: "The anger of the Lord is kindled.” In this connection discuss also the significance of our lack of capacity for sustained indignation against sin. 

4. What are the steps in apostasy? What idols does the modern man set up in place of God? 

5. What are the ordinances of the gospel? Discuss the relations between these ordinances and the establishment of righteousness. 

6. What were the reasons prompting the coming forth of the Restoration movement? How fully has the church justified her existence in light of this statement of purpose? What creative suggestions have you to offer in this connection? 

7. For what purposes were the revelations given? How does divine revelation promote realization of these purposes? 

8. Enumerate some of the warnings in this revelation. Why is this note of warning sounded so clearly in the preface in this book?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

There is no uncertainty here, but strong notes of authority and of warning, of universal purpose and of the awfulness of apostasy. We shall do well to capture these dominant notes at the beginning of our course of study. 

The early Saints were no triflers, but men of sober intent. We have been endowed with far greater gifts than they, but many of these gifts are dissipated because we lack the high seriousness.

LESSON FOUR 

Revelations Concerning the Book of Mormon 

Introduction: 

(Sections 2, 3, 5, 15)

The first task of Joseph in connection with the Restoration was the translation and publication of the Book of Mormon. The earlier revelations naturally have to do with this important work. 

Major Emphases:

The method and means of translation. 

The purpose of the Book of Mormon. 

use. The testimony of the witnesses. 

1. Describe the Urim and Thummim, and tell something of its history and 

2. Was the work of translation automatic, or did the Lord use Joseph's own personality as fully as possible? Give reasons for your answer. Consider in this connection the statement "If there are faults, they are the mistakes of men."1 

3. Who was Martin Harris? What was his connection with the Book of 

Mormon? With what major disaster was he connected? 

4. In what way was Joseph responsible for the loss of the Book of Mormon manuscript? In what way was he punished? What important principles were emphasized in connection with this incident? What scriptural parallel is there for this experience? 

5. Who were the "three witnesses"? Who were the "eight witnesses?"

Why were the Book of Mormon plates shown only to these persons? Did any of these witnesses ever renounce his testimony? 

6. Describe the various sets of Book of Mormon plates. What other ancient things were found with them? 

7. What is the purpose of the Book of Mormon? What is its relation to the other Standard Books (42: 5)? How may this purpose be more fully achieved? 

8. Who were Joseph's chief assistants in the work of translation? What became of the Book of Mormon manuscript? What became of the plates? 

Present Spiritual Significance: 

In the work of translation the Lord used Joseph as fully as possible. When 

He endows us with His Spirit today He will have similar regard for our personality.

In every age God has been willing to commit the truth into the hands of faithful men, and to trust them to promulgate it.

Introduction: 

LESSON FIVE 

Instructions to Early Converts (Sections 4, 6, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14)

A group of earnest and spiritually minded men soon surrounded Joseph. 

Each wanted some guidance in regard to his part in the movement. The revelations given in answer to their inquiries cover common ground and show considerable duplication, and they are therefore considered together. 

Major Emphases: "The field is white, "Say nothing "A marvelous work is about to come forth." all ready to harvest." "Whosoever will . . . is called." but repentance." "No one can assist in this work, except he shall be hum ble, and full of love."

1. In what way is the Book of Mormon "a marvelous work"? In what sense may the Restoration movement be properly regarded as "a marvelous work?" 

2. Give illustrations of the missionary emphasis in these revelations. Why was missionary work specially important at this time? What type of missionary work was done by these young men? 

3. Discuss, briefly, the statement that "whosoever will thrust in his sickle and reap is called of God?" What is the relation between this statement and "Blessed are they which do hunger and thirst after righteousness: for they shall be filled?"1 

4. In what great Cause were these men enlisted? Is this Cause a later discovery or is it an essential part of the Restoration movement? What surrounding circumstances made this Cause especially attractive to these young men? 

5. When were these young men to begin to preach? What was to be the heart of their message? Explain in a few words what this message means for today. 

6. Enumerate some of the promises made in these revelations. What is the greatest gift mentioned here? 

Are these ap- 

7. What admonitions are contained in these revelations? plicable today? Give reasons for your answer. 

8. What are the fundamental conditions of effective service in the kingdom of God? Explain the meaning of each of these conditions. 

Present Spiritual Significance: 

The note of serious purpose which runs through these revelations is vital to effective ministry today. Each one of these communications, or all together, might well be read as a great charge to the priesthood in their several tasks. 

The greatest need of our generation, as of theirs, is the need for repentance.

Introduction: 

LESSON SIX Apostolic Ministry (Section 16) 

In a special sense apostolic witness is borne by men ordained to the Quorum of Twelve, but in a larger sense it is borne by the church as a whole. 

It was eminently fitting that even before the church was organized this fact should be made clear. 

Major Emphases:

The importance of repentance and baptism. 

The choice of the Twelve.

The work of the Twelve. 

1. Why is it important that the leaders of the church shall "rely upon the things which are written?" How may we do this and yet avoid stagnation?

How will this help us to avoid spiritual instability? 

2. Why was it important that these young men should not rush into premature organization? What held them back from such a step? 

3. Were Oliver and David ever members of the Quorum of Twelve? Name some other apostles who are not members of the Quorum of Twelve. 

4. What is the basic apostolic task? Who shares in this task? Discuss its importance in the total life of the church. 

5. What obligations are laid on the Twelve in this revelation? In what degree are these obligations shared by other ministers? 

6. What is the meaning of the statement "Contend against no church, save it be the church of the devil?" Discuss this in light of the statement that "their creeds are an abomination" and of the teaching of Jesus.¹ 

7. The apostolic message was not complete until it included baptism as well as repentance. Why? Discuss the importance of baptism as a social witness to repentance, and as the point at which repentance is organized for kingdom building. 

8. Who were the first members of the Quorum of Twelve? When were they ordained? Who are the present members of this Quorum?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

As in ancient days, the church has three fundamental ministries: preaching, teaching and healing. Of these the first in point of time and the most fundamental in its significance, is the ministry by which men are called to repentance and baptism. On this solid foundation, and on no other, can the kingdom be built.

LESSON SEVEN What We Believe (Section 17: 1-6) Introduction: 

The statement of fundamental beliefs contained in this section is not exhaustive, since it does not mention the Cause of Zion and other important items of doctrine, but it does state clearly the nature of God, our need of salvation, the fact of atonement and the possibility of redemption. This is the foundation of Latter Day Saint theology. 

Major Emphases: ...

The attributes of Divinity. The gift of the Son. The fall. “Justification and sanctification through the grace of our Lord and Saviour 

Jesus Christ."

1. Recount, briefly, the historical background of section 17. What evidences taken from the section itself indicate that it is a compilation from several revelations? 

2. What are the attributes of divinity mentioned in this section? What do we mean when we state that "God made man in his own image?" Is this true physically or spiritually, or both? 

3. What do we mean by the "fall of man"? In what sense is the fall a present experience? 

4. What were the major aspects of the ministry of Jesus? State, in a few sentences, the importance of each for us today. 

5. What do we mean by "the atonement"? life that the Son of God loved you enough to what sin does to the innocent? 

What does it mean in your show you in His own body 

What must pre- 

6. In what way is baptism related to the atonement? cede baptism in order to make it effective? What must follow it? 

7. When was the gospel first preached? For whom is it available? 

Why is it important to us that we shall believe this? 

8. What do we mean by "justification"? What do we mean by "sanctification"? In what sense are these accomplished "through the grace of 

Jesus Christ"?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

We shall do well to make room in our private and public worship for careful consideration of this basic statement of our theology in the spirit of the Master, so that our souls may be enlarged by contemplating His majesty and. His great purpose for us.

Introduction: 

LESSON EIGHT The Organization of the Church 

(Sections 7: 26; 20: 1; 26; 28)

The fundamental principles on which the church was organized were clearly stated at the very beginning. There have been no essential variations from these fundamentals, and they are still as important as they were a century ago. 

Major Emphases:

The organization meeting. the conditions of effective baptism. the fundamental ministries . . . the sacrament . .

agency. 

1. When was the church organized? Who participated in this organization? What were their respective ages? 

2. What are the fundamental conditions of effective baptism? Discuss, briefly, the importance of each of these. 

3. Were converts permitted to enter the church in any other way than baptism? Why? Are these reasons still valid? 

4. What is the divine instruction regarding the relative time of baptism and confirmation? What is the reason for this instruction? 

5. When was the sacrament of the Lord's supper first administered? What instructions are given regarding the sacrament?? What is the meaning of the phrase "partake unworthily"? 

6. What are the fundamental tasks of the ministry? What do we mean by preaching? Teaching? Expounding? Exhorting? Inviting? 

7. According to what principle are members of the priesthood ordained ?3 

What are the duties of the elders? Priests? Teachers? Deacons? 

8. What members of the priesthood have ex-officio rights in conference? 

Why? What is the basic law for participation of the membership in church life?4

Present Spiritual Significance: 

Careful consideration of the fundamental conditions of church membership, and of the purpose of ministry to church members, will enrich every sacrament service and make it effective for the achievement of the purpose of God in organizing the church. 10

Introduction: 

LESSON NINE 

The Revelation to Martin Harris (Section 18) Martin Harris was a good man, but he was bewildered. The assurances given him in this revelation were sufficient for his needs, and he went ahead with his commitments regarding the Book of Mormon and gave valuable service in other ways. 

Major Emphases:

The dignity of the work of God. . . . The nature of eternal punishment. 

The sin of covetousness. The call to personal testimony. . . . The joy of salvation.

1. What is the historical background of this revelation? Why was Martin

Harris so concerned about the matters raised here? 

2. Why does this revelation start out with an affirmation of the nature and work of Christ? What would be the effect of this statement in the life of a man who was so gravely concerned over the possible loss of a few dollars? 

What is the significance of this approach in teaching the financial law? 

3. "Surely every man must repent or suffer" (18:1). Is this true? 

Justify and illustrate your answer. 

4. What is the meaning of the phrase "eternal punishment"? What is the meaning of the phrase "eternal judgment”? 

5. Are rewards and punishment in the moral realm added from without, or are they the natural resultants of our acts? Discuss this briefly. 

6. What constitutes adequate confession of sin? What are the values of such confession? 

7. What is the meaning of covetousness? How may a man covet his own property? Enumerate some of the forms in which covetousness is manifested today? 

8. What specific instructions were given Martin Harris in this revelation? 

What is the principle behind each of them? What is the relation between effective prayer, effective testimony, and the elimination of covetousness? 

Present Spiritual Significance: 

We are stewards over our own property. This requires that we shall manage this property for God. It also requires that in times of crisis we shall make special sacrifices "for the work's sake.” 11

Introduction: 

LESSON TEN 

Joseph and the Church (Sections 19; 27; 29: 2; 43: 1-4)

Joseph naturally took the place of leadership when the church was organized but his relations with the other brethren and with the church had to be worked out in actual situations. The major revelations covering this matter were received during the first year of our church experience. 

Major Emphases: 

Joseph is the mouthpiece of God to the church (19: 1; 27: 2; 43: 1, 2).

The church is to accept his leadership "by common consent" and "by the prayer of faith" (27: 4). . . . He is to receive support in temporal and spiritual things (43: 3). . . . The elders are to "instruct and edify each other" in the commandments and in their application to the necessities of church life (43: 3, 4).

1. What was the procedure at the meeting when the church was organized? Who were present at this meeting? What priesthood did these men hold? 

2. By what primary right did Joseph take the lead of the group? What right did the church give him? What titles are here applied to Joseph? What does each of them mean? 

3. What was the relation of Joseph to the "cause of Zion"? What is the connection between this Cause and other church activities? 

4. Does the instruction given to the church on the day of organization involve blind acceptance of the prophetic word? What self-discipline does it involve? 

5. In what respect was the situation of Joseph and Oliver akin to that of Moses and Aaron? How long was Joseph to remain in this position of prophetic leadership? What provision was made for his successor? 

6. Why was it required that Joseph should preside over the conference "by common consent"?" Under the circumstances what was the meaning of "common consent"? What is the relation between "common consent" and the law and commandments? 

7. In what ways were the Saints called on to support Joseph? Why?

Discuss, briefly, Doctrine and Covenants 125: 14 in this connection. Restate in your own words the principles herein involved. 

8. Under what conditions are the priesthood promised added glory? Discuss this in relation to mutual aid and prophetic leadership (43: 3, 4). 

Present Spiritual Significance: 

The primary responsibility of the prophet and of all other church leaders is to God. They are required to speak the truth as kindly yet as clearly as possible. For their own sake, and for the sake of the work, the ministry must become acquainted with the laws and covenants of the church, and must be given spiritual support in teaching them. 12

introduction: 

LESSON ELEVEN 

The Antiquity of the Gospel (Sections 7, 26)

The early Saints saw the purpose of divinity as one great whole, in life or in death, in time and in eternity. With such a faith they regarded the 

Restoration as the culmination of the hopes and labors of the men of other days, the "dispensation of the fullness of times." 

Major Emphases:

The continuing ministry of the ancient prophets . . . the keys of the dispensations . . . the restoration of all things.1

1. Is it reasonable to believe that the gospel was known among men prior to the coming of Jesus Christ? Justify your answer from the Scriptures. 

2. What great tragedy closed every dispensation both before and after 

Christ? What are the steps in apostasy? Was any residue of spiritual enlightenment left in the world from the successive dispensations? 

3. What happens to the priesthood of a godly minister at his death? 

Does his interest in the building of the kingdom continue? Give reasons for your answer. 

4. What was the relation of John the Baptist to the Restoration movement? 

Of Moroni? Of Peter, James, and John? 

5. What is the meaning of the statement that the hearts of the fathers must be turned to the children, and the children to the fathers? In what ways can the ministry of today help in this work? 

6. What is the meaning of the term "dispensation"? What do we mean by the "dispensation of the fullness of time"? Suggest some Biblical phrases which have a related meaning. 

7. What was the effect of belief in the dispensation of the fullness of time on the attitude of the Saints toward the authority of the priesthood? 

Toward the responsibility of the priesthood? Toward the message of the priesthood? 

8. What was the probable result of this belief on the attitude of the 

Saints in times of persecution? What did it do to their fear of death? What did it do to their fear of defeat? What did it do to their hope of the kingdom?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

The drama of every day is played against the background of eternity. The salvation of men and the building of the kingdom are not new ideas, but have been in the heart of God from the beginning. If such a God as ours is for us, who can be against us? 13

LESSON TWELVE The Revelations to Moses and to Enoch Introduction: 

(Sections 22, 36)

Both of these revelations were given Joseph in connection with the Inspired revision of the Holy Scriptures. Their major significance lies in their portrayal of the concern of divinity for the well-being of mankind, and in the majestic sweep of the creative purpose they reveal. 

Major Emphases:

The concern of divinity for mankind. . . . The temptation to worship 

Satan. . . . The source of strength. . . . The extent of Zion and purpose of creation.

1. What is the historical background of the revelation to Moses? Where was it first published? What of the revelation to Enoch? 

2. Name various men who have seen God face to face. By what power is this possible? Is the presence of the glory of God an emotional experience, an illuminating experience, or both? Discuss this briefly. 

3. Why was the presence of God withdrawn from Moses? What was the result? Do you think this part of the revelation was especially valuable to 

Joseph? Why? 

4. Why would not Moses worship Satan? What is the result of worshiping false gods? Illustrate this in terms of modern temptations. 

5. In what ways was the city of Enoch different from other cities? What happened to this city? What will yet happen to the people of Enoch? 

6. What revelation did Enoch receive regarding the suffering of God? 

Discuss this briefly. 

7. What future events were predicted by Enoch? icance for us? 

What is their signif- 

8. What do we mean by immortality? What is the meaning of eternal life? Express in your own words the purpose of God in creation.

Present Spiritual Significance: 

Men are always called on to choose whom they will worship, and evil always parades in an attractive guise. We can safely put our trust in the spirit that leads us to promote man's eternal well-being. In so doing we can find strength to combat evil, and the assurance of eventual triumph. 14

Introduction: 

LESSON THIRTEEN 

Personal Testimony (Sections 4: 1; 12: 4; 13: 3; 14: 3; 29: 2; 30: 3; 32:1-3, 33; 35: 2, 3) 

A dominant note of the early revelations was the importance of personal testimony. Even when regularly organized missionary journeys were taken the prevailing emphasis was still on personal testimony rather than on formal preaching. 

Major Emphases: "The field is white already to harvest" (6: 2). . . . "Seek to bring forth and establish the cause of Zion" (6: 3). . . . “Declare repentance unto this people" (13: 3; 14: 3). . . . Keep the commandments. . . . "Give heed to that which is written” (31: 1). . . . The wide call to ministry (35: 3).

1. What were the values of a strong missionary emphasis to the early church? To the missionaries? To their friends? 

2. What was the goal of early missionary work? What is the relation between repentance and the Cause of Zion? 

3. What part did the Scriptures play in the lives of the early missionaries? 

What specific commands were given in this connection (31: 1)? 

4. Enumerate some of the conditions of missionary success named in the early revelations. Discuss their applicability today. 

5. Discuss, briefly, the command: "Contend against no church save it be the church of the devil." 

6. Quote phrases from these revelations which indicate the urgency of the missionary call. Is this call still urgent? Discuss this briefly. 

7. Indicate the value of the Book of Mormon in missionary work; of the Cause of Zion; of definite fundamental principles; of the idea of modern revelation. 

8. Who participated in early missionary work? What missionary method was most favored? What great value did this method have? Is it suited to modern proselyting?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

The early Saints held deep convictions fearlessly and were well grounded in the Scriptures. They testified from warm hearts as well as from informed minds. Missionary work was not the task of a few but the privilege of the many. Their missionary gains are matched today wherever we find their missionary zeal paralleled. 15

Introduction: 

LESSON FOURTEEN Magnify Thine Office (Section 23) 

As the number of church members grew, Joseph and Oliver found themselves increasingly occupied in the work of leadership. The time inevitably came when they must arrange for the support of their families. In this crisis they were told to do the work of their ministry with confident expectation of adequate support. 

Major Emphases:

The Saints are to be Joseph is to give all his strength to Zion. blessed in supporting him and Oliver in their work. . . . In extending the kingdom they are not to rely on miracles, but on the power of testimony. 

1. What special crisis made this revelation necessary? Where was it given and to whom was it addressed? What is its importance for us today? 

2. What important work were Joseph and Oliver doing at this time? 

What other responsibilities did they carry? 

3. Why was Joseph instructed to depend on his ministry for his support? 

What obligation did this place on him? What obligation did it place on the 

Saints? What blessings were promised with these obligations? 

4. What very helpful preparation did Joseph make for preaching? Is guidance in preaching available for those who make no study of the Scriptures? Is it available for those whose minds are exclusively occupied with other affairs? 

5. Why were Joseph and Oliver advised to "require not miracles except 

I shall command you"? Discuss, briefly, the place of "signs and wonders" in such a missionary situation. 

6. What legal difficulty was Joseph having at this time? What condemnation is pronounced on those who use the law as a means of oppression? Why? 

7. Why was it important for the Saints to take care of the families of missionary elders? How is this principle carried out today? 

8. What is meant by leaving a testimony against those who fail to receive the ministry? Does this involve resentment and vengeance, or solemn warning? Discuss this briefly.

Present Spiritual Significance: 

In view of the need of humanity, the revelation of God to these first elders was "magnify thine office.” Circumstances are not such as to require every elder to give his full time to the work of his ministry, but they do require a similar basic devotion. 1.6

Introduction: 

LESSON FIFTEEN 

Emma Smith and the Women of Zion (Sections 24, 82)

The pioneer women of the early church were primarily concerned with their homes and children, the support of the ministry, and such aids as social service and the improvement of the quality of church life. Emma Smith, wife of Joseph, was their natural leader. 

Major Emphases: "Thou shalt have an inheritance in Zion" (24: 1): "Be a comfort unto my servant thy husband" (24:2). . . . "Make a selection of sacred hymns" (24: 3). ... "Widows and orphans shall be provided for" (82: 2).

1. Tell in a few words the story of Emma Smith and her part in the work of the church prior to this time. What is your estimate of her character? 

2. Under what conditions was Emma promised an inheritance in Zion? 

Why was she especially concerned about this at this time? Discuss, briefly, the devotion which made it possible for her to give her husband to the work of the church? 

3. What were the specific things which Emma had not seen and which are here referred to? Discuss, briefly, the principle involved in the command, "murmur not because of the things which thou hast not seen.” 

4. In what way did Emma fulfill the command to support her husband? 

Name some other church women who have thus shared the ministry of their husbands. 

5. In what sense was Emma an "elect lady"? For what purpose was she "ordained"? 

6. What special task is Emma given in this revelation? When was it fulfilled? In what way does its influence reach to our own days? 

7. Enumerate, briefly, the laws of the church concerning women and children.¹ 

8. What are the provisions of the church for the protection of the families of deceased stewards? Is the law in this regard still operative?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

The work of the sisters in building zionic homes, their partnership in ministerial endeavor, and their contribution to the finer aspects of our worship and social experience, are still of major significance. There is place in the church for a constantly larger number of "elect ladies." 17

Introduction: 

LESSON SIXTEEN 

Anticipations of Zion (Sections 27: 3; 28: 2; 36; 37; 38; 45: 12, 13)

The Cause of Zion was dear to the heart of the Saints from the very beginning, and was a dominant factor in determining the methods and content of their missionary work, and in shaping the lives of the converts after baptism. All looked forward eagerly for the place of gathering to be designated. 

Major Emphases:

The city is to be built on the frontier (27:3). It is to be built by the gathering of the elect (28:2). . . . The Saints are to be strengthened and prepared against the time of this gathering (30: 3). . . . It is to be the culmination of the dream of the ages (36), a great missionary center (38:7), a place of true riches (38: 4, 9), and a place of safety from impending disaster (45: 12, 13).

1. What meaning did the early Saints attach to the phrase the "Cause of Zion"? Why were they especially interested in such a Cause? 

2. What early intimation was given concerning the location of Zion? Why was it desirable that the Saints should gather on the frontier? 

3. What influence did the gathering have on the places where missionary work was carried on? What was its influence on the content of the message and the urgency of the preaching? 

4. Discuss, briefly, the influence of the hope of Zion on local church organization (section 30: 3). 

5. The revelations state that "the Lord called his people Zion, because they were of one heart and one mind and dwelt in righteousness." Explain this. 

6. What reasons were given for the preliminary gathering at Ohio (38: 7)? 

7. Enumerate and discuss some of the promises given to the Saints in connection with the gathering. 

8. Trace the unfolding of the zionic idea in the first year of the life of the church. What outstanding characteristics of the kingdom were emphasized during this period?

Present Spiritual Significance: 

The idea of the gathering took on practical significance as the Saints sought to build. Many of the details have yet to be filled in. This will be done as we move forward in faith, and grapple with the practical problems of gathering. 18

Introduction: 

LESSON SEVENTEEN The Basic Law 

(Sections 41, 42)

The Saints came to Kirtland to be together, but they also had to have a meeting of minds and hearts before the basic law of the church could be revealed to them. 

Major Emphases: 

The missionary task. must teach the principles. erty as His stewards. The

The ministry must be regularly ordained, and Those who love God will manage their prop- Standard Books are to guide the growth of the church. . . . Procedure in eliminating internal friction.

1. What is the first great commandment given the church? In what ways does the subsequent growth of the church depend upon the quality of missionary endeavor? 

2. Why was it important that the ministry should be "regularly ordained"?

Discuss briefly the significance of the command "if you receive not the spirit, ye shall not teach." 

3. What is the church law with regard to murder? Marital fidelity? Gossip? 

4. What principles are here laid down with regard to the use of property? What is the motivation for the practice of stewardship? 

5. For what purpose should surplus property be consecrated? May surplus which has been consecrated be later reclaimed? Why?

Scriptures? Under what conditions will the Scriptures be augmented from time to time?
